Schur Visibility and Anti-Phantom Reduction in One-Component Navier–Stokes Degeneration
Runlong Yu

Abstract
We study the finite-scale one-component degeneration problem for suitable weak solutions of the three-dimensional incompressible Navier–Stokes equations under a scale-invariant bound and smallness of the vertical component. Qualitative compactness gives convergence, in the harmonic-pressure quotient, toward the strict two-and-a-half-dimensional boundary, but it does not provide a quantitative rate. This paper proves, in an explicitly abstract trace-obstruction skeleton associated with the old observable closure, that the standard old observable package is insufficient to force a logarithmic or power selected-trace rate. The negative result is an envelope/skeleton theorem, not a Navier–Stokes counterexample. After excluding elementary high-frequency escape by parabolic trace drop and fixed-window analytic obstruction by finite-dimensional Lojasiewicz control, the remaining obstruction is an all-order finite-mode flat branch. We identify the Navier–Stokes-specific mechanism needed to control this branch: strict Schur trace-projectability may fail, but the resulting defect can be visible through the relaxed vertical-pressure channel. In active finite-window models, strict Schur phantoms are relaxed-visible. The final theorem is a conditional dichotomy: either relaxed anti-phantom closure holds and yields conditional logarithmic strict-shadow selection, or there exists an NS-realizable, cleaned, relaxed-invisible, unaligned left-singular cascade.



Keywords.
Navier–Stokes equations; suitable weak solutions; one-component regularity; strict 2.5D degeneration; old observables; Schur obstruction; vertical pressure; trace cost; vertical duality.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.
35Q30; 35B65; 35B45; 76D05.

1
Introduction and theorem status

1.1
Problem and compactness boundary

Let

Q_{r}(z_{0})=B_{r}(x_{0})\times(t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}),\qquad z_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0}),

and write
Q_{r}=Q_{r}(0,0)
. We consider suitable weak solutions
(u,p)
of

\partial_{t}u-\Delta u+(u\cdot\nabla)u+\nabla p=0,\qquad\nabla\cdot u=0.

(1.1)

in
Q_{1}
. The weak-solution framework used here is the local suitable-weak theory descending from Leray–Hopf weak solutions and the Scheffer–Caffarelli–Kohn–Nirenberg partial regularity program
[
1
,
2
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
. We also use the later local compactness, pressure, and
\varepsilon
-regularity refinements developed in, among others,
[
11
,
14
,
15
,
24
,
31
,
26
,
25
,
34
,
40
]
. The scale-invariant regime is

\Phi(1)\leq M,\qquad C_{3}(1)=\delta\ll 1,

where
C_{3}
measures the vertical component
u_{3}
. If
\delta\to 0
under the fixed bound
\Phi(1)\leq M
, compactness forces subsequential convergence, modulo harmonic pressures, toward the strict two-and-a-half-dimensional boundary

V=(V_{h},0),\qquad\nabla_{h}\cdot V_{h}=0,

with pressure satisfying
\partial_{3}Q=0
. The rate problem asks for more: whether the data

\Phi(1)\leq M,\qquad C_{3}(1)=\delta

force a quantitative approximation

X^{\mathrm{har}}_{\theta}(u,p;M)\leq F_{M,\theta}(\delta)

with
F_{M,\theta}
logarithmic or algebraic as
\delta\downarrow 0
.

This question is adjacent to, but not the same as, the classical Prodi–Serrin continuation theory and related pressure/Morrey/initial-time regularity mechanisms
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
13
,
17
,
18
,
23
,
30
]
. It is also informed by one-component, one-gradient, and anisotropic regularity criteria
[
19
,
21
,
22
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
32
,
33
,
35
,
37
,
39
]
. The finite-dimensional obstruction language below is closer to an analytic-geometric reduction than to a direct PDE regularity proof, and is therefore naturally compared with singular-set and approximation techniques such as
[
16
,
20
]
.

The paper treats this rate question as an observability problem. The central message is

\boxed{\text{old observables do not force a logarithmic or power selected-trace rate,}}

and

\boxed{\text{the missing structure is Navier--Stokes-specific vertical-pressure observability.}}

The phrase “old observables” refers to the local quantities produced by the standard compactness and preparation arguments: scale-invariant energy-pressure quantities, harmonic-pressure quotient distances, covariance stresses, unresolved variance, prepared residuals, good-time trace functionals, finite-window compatibility quotients, and trace-cost duality. The pressure and harmonic-gauge part is aligned with the local pressure literature
[
9
,
36
,
41
]
, while the covariance/coarse-graining language is informed by commutator and energy-flux methods such as
[
12
]
.

1.2
Theorem status and non-claims

The statements in the paper have different logical status. The following table is meant to prevent overclaiming.

Statement

Status

Qualitative strict-shadow compactness

standard compactness statement recalled here

Old-observable no-rate theorem

proved abstract trace-skeleton/envelope-insufficiency theorem

Trace-drop removes high-frequency escape

proved under sharp admissible-time intersection

Fixed-window analytic obstruction control

proved finite-dimensional analytic theorem

All-order non-summable finite-stage model

proved model theorem

Active finite-window Schur relaxed visibility

proved periodic finite-window theorem

Localized relaxed visibility

proved under explicit perturbative or controlled finite-window hypotheses

Final anti-phantom dichotomy

conditional reduction and theorem target

Unconditional one-component regularity

not claimed

Navier–Stokes counterexample

not claimed

The main negative result is an insufficiency theorem for an envelope strictly larger than the true Navier–Stokes image. It does not construct a singular Navier–Stokes solution. The final logarithmic regularity-radius conclusion is conditional on prepared comparison, sharp finite-window reduction, and vertical-duality or relaxed anti-phantom closure.

The word
proved
is used below only for statements whose hypotheses explicitly include the structural inputs needed in the proof. Statements involving localization, moving windows, combined observability, NS residual representation, or anti-phantom closure are formulated as conditional reductions unless those hypotheses are part of the theorem itself.

1.3
Main theorems

The paper is organized around four statements.

Theorem 1.1

(Old-observable no-rate theorem)
.

For every sequence
a_{n}\downarrow 0
, the abstract old trace-obstruction skeleton associated with the old-observable closure contains a branch with selected-time trace distance
m_{n}=\frac{1}{2}a_{n}^{2}
, while all fixed finite-window residuals, covariance defects, variance quantities, pressure-gauge observables, good-time observables, and prepared residuals are controlled or vanish. Consequently, from the old observable package alone, without a uniform finite-window inverse, a quantitative compactness modulus, trace-drop intersection, or vertical-duality/relaxed anti-phantom input, no universal logarithmic or power selected-trace rate follows.

Theorem 1.2

(Obstruction separation)
.

After parabolic trace-drop and sharp admissible-time intersection, pure high-frequency escape is excluded. After restriction to any fixed analytic finite window, finite-dimensional Lojasiewicz control excludes arbitrary-slow convergence inside that fixed window. The remaining abstract obstruction is an all-order finite-mode flat branch with potentially non-summable finite-stage exactification constants.

Theorem 1.3

(Schur-to-relaxed visibility)
.

In a clean active periodic finite-window model, let
\Phi_{\Lambda}=\Ker A_{\Lambda}^{*}
be the strict trace-invisible Schur cokernel and let

J^{\mathrm{rel}}_{\Lambda}s=\sum_{\sigma\in\Lambda}\mu_{\sigma}s_{\sigma}w_{\sigma},\qquad\mu_{\sigma}=\frac{|\sigma_{h}|^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2}}{|\sigma_{h}|^{2}}.

If
\Lambda
is active, meaning
|\sigma_{h}|\neq 0
and
|\sigma_{3}|\neq 0
for every output mode, then

J^{\mathrm{rel}}_{\Lambda}s=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s=0.

Thus a strict-invisible Schur defect need not be a true Navier–Stokes phantom; in active finite windows it is relaxed-visible through vertical pressure.

Theorem 1.4

(Final conditional dichotomy)
.

After preparation, good-time selection, finite-window reduction, strict–Schur obstruction removal, harmonic-pressure cleaning, localized coercivity, homogeneous-tail testing, and NS-realizability filtering, exactly one of the following remains. Either the surviving NS-derived residuals satisfy relaxed anti-phantom closure, which yields controlled trace-cost exactification and conditional logarithmic strict-shadow selection; or there exists an NS-realizable, cleaned, relaxed-invisible, unaligned left-singular cascade. This cascade is the final obstruction isolated by the present route.

1.4
Main contributions

The paper has five contributions.
First, it isolates the old observable package and formalizes it as a defect-shadow envelope. Second, it proves that the associated abstract trace-obstruction skeleton permits arbitrary-slow selected-trace convergence, so old observables alone cannot give a logarithmic or power rate. Third, it separates false obstructions: parabolic trace-drop removes high-frequency escape, while finite-dimensional analytic geometry removes fixed-window arbitrary-slow behavior. Fourth, it identifies Schur trace-projectability as the remaining strict trace-cost obstruction and shows that active Schur defects are relaxed-visible through vertical pressure. Fifth, it gives a final anti-phantom dichotomy reducing the rate problem to the exclusion or construction of an NS-realizable, cleaned, relaxed-invisible, unaligned left-singular cascade.

1.5
Logical spine and organization

The dependency chain is

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{small }u_{3}+\text{scale bound}\Rightarrow\text{strict compactness}\Rightarrow\text{old envelope}\Rightarrow\text{no-rate branch},\\[2.5pt]
\text{trace-drop + finite-window tests}\Rightarrow\text{finite-mode flat branch}\Rightarrow\text{vertical lift / Schur visibility},\\[2.5pt]
\text{vertical-pressure observability}\Rightarrow\text{final anti-phantom dichotomy}.\end{array}

Section 2 fixes the compactness boundary. Section 3 defines the old observables and the selected-time target. Sections 4 and 5 prove the old-envelope no-rate theorem. Section 6 separates the false escape mechanisms. Section 7 gives trace-cost duality. Section 8 introduces Navier–Stokes realizability and vertical lift cost. Section 9 formulates reduced vertical duality and Schur trace-projectability. Section 10 proves Schur-to-relaxed visibility in active finite windows. Section 11 records the localization, gauge, NS-realizability, and moving-window filters. Section 12 states the final anti-phantom dichotomy. Section 13 gives the conditional logarithmic consequence. Section 14 summarizes the theorem targets. The appendices contain the longer Fourier, localization, and moving-window calculations.

2
Preliminaries and the compactness boundary

2.1
Suitable weak solutions

We use the standard local class. A pair
(u,p)
is a suitable weak solution of (
1.1
) in a parabolic cylinder
Q
if

u\in L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}(Q)\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{x}(Q),\qquad p\in L^{3/2}(Q),

(u,p)
solves (
1.1
) distributionally,
\nabla\cdot u=0
, and for every nonnegative
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(Q)
,

\displaystyle\int|u(t)|^{2}\varphi(t)\,dx

\displaystyle+2\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int|\nabla u|^{2}\varphi\,dx\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int|u|^{2}(\partial_{s}\varphi+\Delta\varphi)\,dx\,ds+\int_{-\infty}^{t}\int(|u|^{2}+2p)u\cdot\nabla\varphi\,dx\,ds

(2.1)

for a.e.
t
. This is the standard suitable-weak class used in the CKN theory and its later refinements
[
8
,
14
,
31
,
26
]
. The pressure is determined only up to functions of time. Locally, after pressure decomposition, it is also natural to quotient by spatially harmonic functions
[
9
,
36
,
41
]
.

2.2
Harmonic pressure gauge

For a cylinder
Q_{\rho}=B_{\rho}\times I_{\rho}
, define

\mathcal{H}(Q_{\rho})=\{h\in L^{3/2}(Q_{\rho}):\Delta h(\cdot,t)=0\text{ in }B_{\rho}\text{ for a.e. }t\}.

We write
[p]_{\mathcal{H}}=p+\mathcal{H}
. The harmonic-pressure excess relative to the strict class is

\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{har}}_{\rho}(u,p;M)=\inf_{(V,Q)\in\mathfrak{S}_{M}(Q_{\rho})}\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}(Q_{\rho})}\left[\rho^{-2}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|u-V|^{3}\,dx\,dt+\rho^{-2}\int_{Q_{\rho}}|p-Q-h|^{3/2}\,dx\,dt\right].

(2.2)

This is the correct local topology for the pressure: the Calderon–Zygmund part is tied to the velocity, while the spatially harmonic part is not controlled by the same local formula; compare the local pressure decompositions in
[
9
,
36
,
31
]
and the harmonic-pressure formulation in
[
41
]
.

2.3
Strict two-and-a-half-dimensional shadows

A strict 2.5D shadow is a pair
(V,Q)
with

V=(V_{h},0),\qquad\nabla_{h}\cdot V_{h}=0,\qquad\partial_{3}Q=0,

solving

\partial_{t}V_{h}-\Delta V_{h}+\nabla_{h}\cdot(V_{h}\otimes V_{h})+\nabla_{h}Q=0.

(2.3)

We denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{M}(Q_{\rho})
the class of such shadows in
Q_{\rho}
with a fixed scale-invariant bound depending on
M
.

The vertical pressure condition
\partial_{3}Q=0
produces a nonlinear compatibility constraint. Formally, taking horizontal divergence in (
2.3
) gives

-\Delta_{h}Q=\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(V_{a}V_{b}),\qquad a,b\in\{1,2\}.

Thus, modulo horizontal harmonic issues and after fixing the horizontal pressure gauge,

Q=-\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(V_{a}V_{b}).

The condition
\partial_{3}Q=0
is equivalent to

\mathcal{C}(V):=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(V_{a}V_{b})=0.

(2.4)

We also write

B(A,B)=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(A_{a}B_{b}),\qquad\mathcal{C}(V)=B(V,V).

(2.5)

At a base shadow
V
, the linearized compatibility map is

D\mathcal{C}_{V}[W]=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(V_{a}W_{b}+W_{a}V_{b}).

(2.6)

The quotient

\operatorname{Obs}_{V}:=\coker D\mathcal{C}_{V}

is the formal obstruction space. A central difficulty is that

W\in\Ker D\mathcal{C}_{V}

does not by itself imply that
W
is tangent to an actual curve in
\mathcal{C}^{-1}(0)
. This is the source of finite-order and flat-branch obstruction phenomena.

2.4
Qualitative compactness versus rate

The standard compactness mechanism gives the following qualitative implication, using local energy compactness, pressure decomposition, and the Aubin–Lions–Simon compactness principle
[
10
,
8
,
31
]
.

Proposition 2.1

(Qualitative strict-shadow compactness)
.

Fix
M\geq 1
and
0<\theta<1/2
. There exists a nondecreasing modulus
\omega_{M,\theta}
with
\omega_{M,\theta}(s)\to 0
as
s\downarrow 0
such that every suitable weak solution in
Q_{1}
satisfying
\Phi(1)\leq M
obeys

\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{har}}_{\theta}(u,p;M)\leq\omega_{M,\theta}(C_{3}(1)).

(2.7)

Sketch.

Assume otherwise. Then there are suitable weak solutions
(u^{(n)},p^{(n)})
with
\Phi^{(n)}(1)\leq M
,
C_{3}^{(n)}(1)\to 0
, and
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{har}}_{\theta}(u^{(n)},p^{(n)};M)\geq\eta_{0}>0
. The local energy bound gives

u^{(n)}\text{ bounded in }L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{2}(Q_{\sigma})\cap L_{t}^{2}H_{x}^{1}(Q_{\sigma})\qquad(\sigma<1).

The equation gives a uniform bound for
\partial_{t}u^{(n)}
in a fixed negative Sobolev space, after localizing in
Q_{\sigma}
, because

\partial_{t}u^{(n)}=\Delta u^{(n)}-\operatorname{div}(u^{(n)}\otimes u^{(n)})-\nabla p^{(n)}

with the three terms bounded respectively by the energy and pressure bounds. Aubin–Lions–Simon compactness
[
10
]
therefore gives, after passing to a subsequence,

u^{(n)}\to V\quad\text{strongly in }L^{3}_{\mathrm{loc}}(Q_{1}),

and the pressure representatives, after subtracting time-dependent constants, converge weakly in
L^{3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Since
u_{3}^{(n)}\to 0
in
L^{3}
, the limit has the form
V=(V_{h},0)
, and incompressibility gives
\nabla_{h}\cdot V_{h}=0
.

For the pressure, use the standard local decomposition on a smaller cylinder
[
8
,
9
,
36
,
31
]
: the Calderon–Zygmund part generated by
u^{(n)}\otimes u^{(n)}
converges strongly in
L^{3/2}
because
u^{(n)}\to V
strongly in
L^{3}
, while the remaining spatially harmonic part is compact in the interior after quotienting by
\mathcal{H}
. Hence the pressure converges in the harmonic-pressure quotient. Passing to the limit in the horizontal equations gives (
2.3
); passing to the limit in the vertical equation gives
\partial_{3}Q=0
. Thus the limit is an admissible strict shadow in the quotient topology, contradicting the lower bound on
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{har}}_{\theta}
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Proposition
2.1
is intentionally qualitative. The compactness argument does not identify a power or logarithmic decay rate for
\omega_{M,\theta}
. The rest of the paper asks which additional structures could produce such a rate.

3
Old observables and the selected-trace target

We now organize the quantities visible in the one-component degeneration problem. An observable is any object naturally defined from the solution, the equation, the pressure gauge, a coarse-graining operation, a strict-shadow comparison, a finite-window projection, or a selected-time minimization. The first layers are classical in partial regularity and pressure theory
[
8
,
14
,
31
,
36
]
; the component and anisotropic layers are motivated by one-component regularity criteria
[
21
,
29
,
32
,
33
,
37
,
39
]
; the rate/modulus layer is closest in spirit to quantitative regularity work such as
[
38
]
.

3.1
Levels of observability

We distinguish four levels.

Level 0: native observables.

These are directly defined from
(u,p)
: the cylinders
Q_{r}
, the scale-invariant quantities
A,E,C,D,C_{3}
, the component quantities
u_{h},u_{3}
, and vorticity components.

Level 1: equation-derived observables.

These come from the equations: the pressure Poisson law, incompressibility
\nabla_{h}\cdot u_{h}=-\partial_{3}u_{3}
, and the vertical momentum equation.

Level 2: operation-derived observables.

These arise after smoothing, projection, pressure decomposition, horizontal Helmholtz correction, and covariance expansion. Examples are
U^{\ell}
,
P^{\ell}
,
\tau^{\ell}
,
\kappa^{\ell}
,
G^{\ell}
, and
\partial_{3}P^{\ell}
.

Level 3: selection and obstruction observables.

These are defined only after strict-shadow comparison, selected-time minimization, blow-up, or finite-window projection. Examples include
m_{\ell}
, normalized trace directions
W_{n}
, finite-window obstruction spaces, and trace-cost quotients.

3.2
Twelve observable families

For later reference, we compress the observable atlas into twelve families.

Family

Representative observables

\mathcal{O}_{1}

Scale observables:
z_{0},r,Q_{r}(z_{0})
, parabolic scale trees.

\mathcal{O}_{2}

Energy-pressure observables:
A,E,C,D,C_{3},\Phi,\Psi
.

\mathcal{O}_{3}

Component/anisotropic observables:
u_{h},u_{3},\nabla_{h}u_{h},\partial_{3}u_{h},\nabla_{h}u_{3},\partial_{3}u_{3},\omega_{h},\omega_{3}
.

\mathcal{O}_{4}

Pressure-gauge observables:
[p]_{\mathcal{H}}
, Calderon–Zygmund pressure, horizontal pressure part, vertical pressure remainder, harmonic oscillation,
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{har}}
.

\mathcal{O}_{5}

Limiting/shadow observables: strict class
\mathfrak{S}_{M}
,
(V,Q)
, strict decay,
\mathcal{C}(V)
,
D\mathcal{C}_{V}
,
B(\cdot,\cdot)
.

\mathcal{O}_{6}

Coarse-preparation observables: smoothing
S_{\ell}u
, prepared field
U^{\ell}
, horizontal correction, divergence defect, smoothing tails.

\mathcal{O}_{7}

Covariance/defect observables: Reynolds stress
\tau^{\ell}
, unresolved variance
\kappa^{\ell}
, gradient variance, prepared residual
G^{\ell}
, vertical pressure defect
\mathcal{V}^{\ell}
.

\mathcal{O}_{8}

Local energy observables: full local energy defect, horizontal defect measure
\mu_{h}^{\ell}
, cutoff errors, small-component errors.

\mathcal{O}_{9}

Good-time and trace observables: good-time sets
G_{\ell}
, thick good times
G^{\sharp}_{\ell}
, selected trace energy
E^{\ell}_{\phi}
, sharp distance
m_{\ell}
, normalized trace directions.

\mathcal{O}_{10}

Obstruction observables:
\operatorname{Obs}_{V}
, finite-order obstructions
\mathcal{O}_{k}
, finite-window projections
P_{\Lambda}\mathcal{O}_{k}
, flatness order.

\mathcal{O}_{11}

Trace-cost/duality observables: active trace map
A
, spaces
H,Y
, residual
g
, cost
\Cost^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)
, adjoint
A^{*}
, vertical-duality ratio.

\mathcal{O}_{12}

Rate/modulus observables: compactness modulus
\omega_{M,\theta}
, best modulus
\Omega^{\mathrm{best}}_{M,\theta}
, power and logarithmic profiles.

The total old observable collection is denoted

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{total}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{12}\mathcal{O}_{j}.

The key point is that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{total}}
is descriptive. It does not assert that the selected trace distance is quantitatively controlled by these quantities.

3.3
The selected-time target

The main Level 3 target is the covariance-calibrated trace distance. Given a prepared horizontal field
U^{\ell}
, unresolved variance
\kappa^{\ell}
, nonnegative cutoff
\phi
, and strict shadow
(V,Q)
, set

E^{\ell}_{\phi}(s;U^{\ell},V)=\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|U^{\ell}(s)-V(s)|^{2}\,dx+\int\phi\kappa^{\ell}(s)\,dx.

(3.1)

For a good-time set
G_{\ell}
, define

m_{\ell}=\inf_{s\in G_{\ell}}\inf_{(V,Q)\in\mathfrak{S}_{M}}E^{\ell}_{\phi}(s;U^{\ell},V).

(3.2)

The central quantitative question is whether the old observables force a subcritical bound of the schematic form

m_{\ell}\leq C_{M}\ell^{\mu}+C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b}.

(3.3)

The no-rate theorem below shows that such a bound is not a formal consequence of the abstract old trace skeleton alone.

4
The old-observable envelope

We now formalize the “old world” as an envelope. It is deliberately larger than the true image of Navier–Stokes under coarse-graining. Its purpose is diagnostic: if a rate fails in this larger world, then any true Navier–Stokes rate must use structure beyond this envelope. The envelope viewpoint combines the scale-invariant CKN quantities
[
8
]
, local pressure quotienting
[
9
,
36
,
41
]
, and covariance/commutator bookkeeping familiar from coarse-grained energy balances
[
12
]
.

Fix once and for all nested cylinders

Q_{\mathrm{sh}}\Subset Q_{\mathrm{prep}}\Subset Q_{1},

a nonnegative spatial cutoff
\phi
, and parameters

M\geq 1,\qquad 0<\theta<1/2,\qquad 0<\ell<\ell_{0},\qquad 0<\delta\leq 1.

Here
\ell
represents a coarse-graining scale and
\delta
represents
C_{3}(1)
.

Definition 4.1

(Old defect-shadow object)
.

An old defect-shadow object is a tuple

\mathcal{U}^{\ell}=(U^{\ell},[P^{\ell}],\tau^{\ell},\kappa^{\ell},G^{\ell},\mathcal{V}^{\ell},\mu_{h}^{\ell}),

(4.1)

where

•

U^{\ell}=(U_{h}^{\ell},0)
is a horizontal vector field;

•

[P^{\ell}]
is a pressure class modulo
\mathcal{H}
;

•

\tau^{\ell}
is a horizontal Reynolds covariance stress;

•

\kappa^{\ell}
is unresolved variance;

•

G^{\ell}
is the prepared non-pressure residual;

•

\mathcal{V}^{\ell}
is the vertical pressure compatibility defect, usually
\partial_{3}P^{\ell}
in a negative norm;

•

\mu_{h}^{\ell}
is a horizontal energy or Onsager defect measure.

Definition 4.2

(Minimal old-observable envelope)
.

We say

\mathcal{U}^{\ell}\in\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},0}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)

if the following axioms hold.

O0. Pressure gauge.

Pressure is observed only through the quotient
[P^{\ell}]=P^{\ell}+\mathcal{H}
. All pressure distances are taken after minimizing over spatially harmonic functions.

O1. Scale-invariant size.

There is
C_{M}
such that

\left\|U^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}+\left\|\nabla U^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}+\left\|U^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{3}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}+\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left\|P^{\ell}-h\right\|_{L^{3/2}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}\leq C_{M}.

O2. Horizontal solenoidal preparation.

We have

U^{\ell}=(U_{h}^{\ell},0),\qquad\nabla_{h}\cdot U_{h}^{\ell}=0.

O3. Prepared covariance-form equation.

In distributions on
Q_{\mathrm{prep}}
,

\partial_{t}U_{h}^{\ell}-\Delta U_{h}^{\ell}+\nabla_{h}\cdot(U_{h}^{\ell}\otimes U_{h}^{\ell})+\nabla_{h}P^{\ell}=-\nabla_{h}\cdot\tau^{\ell}+G^{\ell}.

(4.2)

Equivalently, the covariance stress is placed on the left-hand flux and
G^{\ell}
is the genuinely small residual.

O4. Residual hierarchy.

There are fixed exponents
a_{0},N,b>0
such that

\left\|\tau^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{3/2}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}\leq C_{M}\ell^{a_{0}},\qquad\left\|G^{\ell}\right\|_{Z^{\prime}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}\leq C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b},\qquad\left\|\mathcal{V}^{\ell}\right\|_{Y^{\prime}(Q_{\mathrm{prep}})}\leq C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b}.

(4.3)

The spaces
Z^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
are fixed negative norms compatible with local energy and pressure-compatibility pairings.

O5. Positive covariance.

For a.e.
(x,t)
,
\tau^{\ell}(x,t)
is a nonnegative horizontal
2\times 2
matrix and

\kappa^{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}\tr\tau^{\ell}\geq 0.

(4.4)

O6. Good-time variance supply.

On a fixed interval
I_{-}\Subset(-1,0)
,

\int_{I_{-}}\int\phi\kappa^{\ell}\,dx\,dt\leq C_{M}\ell^{2}.

(4.5)

The good-time set

G_{\ell}=\left\{s\in I_{-}:\int\phi\kappa^{\ell}(x,s)\,dx\leq C_{G}\ell^{2}\right\}

(4.6)

satisfies
|G_{\ell}|\geq c_{G}>0
.

O7. Weak horizontal defect balance.

There are terms
D_{h}^{\ell}\geq 0
,
E^{\ell}_{\mathrm{cut}}
, and
E^{\ell}_{\delta}
such that, distributionally in time,

\frac{d}{dt}\int\phi\kappa^{\ell}+\int\phi D_{h}^{\ell}+\int\phi\,d\mu_{h}^{\ell}=-\int\phi\tau^{\ell}:\nabla_{h}U_{h}^{\ell}+E^{\ell}_{\mathrm{cut}}+E^{\ell}_{\delta},

(4.7)

with

\int|E^{\ell}_{\mathrm{cut}}|\leq C_{M}\ell^{a_{0}},\qquad\int|E^{\ell}_{\delta}|\leq C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b},\qquad\int\phi\,d(\mu_{h}^{\ell})_{-}\leq C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b}.

(4.8)

O8. Rough harmonic strict-shadow compactness.

There is a compactness modulus
\Omega_{M}(s)\to 0
as
s\downarrow 0
such that

\dist_{\mathrm{har}}\bigl((U^{\ell},P^{\ell}),\mathfrak{S}_{M}(Q_{\mathrm{sh}})\bigr)\leq\Omega_{M}(\ell^{a_{0}}+\ell^{-N}\delta^{b}).

(4.9)

This is only a qualitative modulus, not a power or logarithmic estimate.

O9. Finite-window compatibility observables.

For every finite frequency window
\Lambda
, there are finite-dimensional spaces
H_{\Lambda},Y_{\Lambda}
, a finite-dimensional active trace-defect map

A_{\Lambda}:H_{\Lambda}\to Y_{\Lambda},

and projected compatibility residuals in
Y_{\Lambda}
. The constants of all finite-dimensional constructions may depend on
\Lambda
. No uniform-in-
\Lambda
inverse or Lojasiewicz constant is assumed.

O10. Selected trace distance.

The quantity

m^{\ell}_{\mathrm{old}}=\inf_{s\in G_{\ell}}\inf_{(V,Q)\in\mathfrak{S}_{M}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|U^{\ell}(s)-V(s)|^{2}\,dx+\int\phi\kappa^{\ell}(s)\,dx\right]

(4.10)

is defined. The envelope does
not
assume a subcritical estimate for
m^{\ell}_{\mathrm{old}}
.

4.1
Enhanced envelopes

The following nested variants will be useful.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\sharp}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},0}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{tr}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\sharp}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{fw}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{tr}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old}+\mathrm{VD}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)

\displaystyle\subset\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{fw}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta).

Here
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\sharp}
includes thick good times,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{tr}}
includes trace tightness for failed normalized branches,
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},\mathrm{fw}}
includes fixed-window exactifiability with constants depending on the window, and
\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old}+\mathrm{VD}}
includes the vertical-duality estimate introduced in
Section

7
.

The true Navier–Stokes image, if all required preparation steps are performed, should satisfy

\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta)\subseteq\mathfrak{D}^{\mathrm{old},0}_{M,\theta}(\ell,\delta),

(4.11)

but it may be a much smaller subset. The main problem is to identify which additional
\mathrm{NS}
-specific constraints cut down the old envelope.

4.2
Explicitly excluded mechanisms

The minimal old envelope does not include any of the following.

(E1)

Vertical-duality active-residual estimate:

|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle|\leq r\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}.

(E2)

Navier–Stokes realizability:
existence of a genuine suitable weak solution
(u,p)
whose coarse-graining produces
\mathcal{U}^{\ell}
.

(E3)

Vertical jet realizability:
existence of vertical perturbation jets
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots
solving the full asymptotic expansion of the three-dimensional equations.

(E4)

Uniform finite-window inverse:
bounds on
A_{\Lambda}^{-1}
independent of
\Lambda
.

(E5)

Lojasiewicz metric regularity:

\dist(x,\mathcal{C}^{-1}(0))\leq C\left\|\mathcal{C}(x)\right\|_{Y}^{\alpha}

with constants uniform across the relevant windows and branches.

(E6)

Subcritical strict-shadow selection:

m^{\ell}_{\mathrm{old}}\leq C_{M}\ell^{\mu}+C_{M}\ell^{-N}\delta^{b}.

These exclusions are essential. The next section shows that, once they are excluded, arbitrary-slow trace convergence is possible in an abstract trace-obstruction skeleton associated with the old envelope.

5
The no-rate theorem in the abstract old trace skeleton

This section gives the first pressure test. We construct an abstract trace branch satisfying the old finite-window and trace-level observable conditions, but whose selected-time trace distance tends to zero at an arbitrarily slow rate. The model is not a Navier–Stokes counterexample and is not claimed to solve the full prepared covariance equation in the genuine PDE class. It is a diagnostic statement about what remains possible after one forgets all mechanisms beyond the old observables.

5.1
The minimal trace-obstruction skeleton

Let

H=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})

be the selected-time trace Hilbert space, with canonical orthonormal basis
(e_{j})_{j\geq 1}
. Let the strict trace class be

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{tr}}=\{0\}\subset H.

This models a neighborhood of a singular strict-shadow base after compressing the strict trace set to its local zero fiber.

Let the compatibility defect space also be

Y=\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}).

Choose a positive sequence
(\lambda_{j})_{j\geq 1}
with

0<\lambda_{j}\downarrow 0,

for instance
\lambda_{j}=\exp(-\exp j)
. Define the quadratic compatibility map

\mathcal{C}:H\to Y,\qquad\mathcal{C}(x)_{j}=\lambda_{j}x_{j}^{2}.

(5.1)

It is a toy version of the strict pressure-compatibility map

\mathcal{C}(V)=B(V,V),

but with degenerating visibility along high trace directions.

Let
P_{\Lambda}
denote the projection onto
\operatorname{span}\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\Lambda}\}
. A fixed finite-window observer sees only
P_{\Lambda}x
and
P_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}(x)
.

Remark 5.1

(Skeleton versus PDE envelope)
.

The space above is the
minimal trace-obstruction skeleton
. It keeps the logical information used by fixed finite-window trace tests, compatibility residuals, and qualitative compactness, but it does not include a genuine parabolic equation, a pressure decomposition in physical space, or a true Navier–Stokes realization map. Thus the theorem below proves an insufficiency statement for the old-observable closure at trace level. Any genuine PDE rate must use some additional structure, such as trace-drop, uniform moving-window control, vertical duality, or relaxed anti-phantom closure.

5.2
The arbitrary-slow branch

Fix a desired trace amplitude sequence

a_{n}\downarrow 0.

Let
0<\mu<1/6
, and let
N,b>0
be the residual-loss exponents in the old envelope. Define the desired subcritical scale

\eta_{n}=\ell_{n}^{\mu}+\ell_{n}^{-N}\delta_{n}^{b}.

(5.2)

To force failure of subcritical selection, choose
\ell_{n}
and
\delta_{n}
so that

\eta_{n}=o(a_{n}^{2}).

(5.3)

For example, set

\ell_{n}=a_{n}^{4/\mu}

and then choose
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
sufficiently fast that

\ell_{n}^{-N}\delta_{n}^{b}\leq a_{n}^{4}.

Then

\eta_{n}\leq 2a_{n}^{4}=o(a_{n}^{2}).

Because
\lambda_{j}\to 0
, one can choose
j_{n}\to\infty
so large that

\lambda_{j_{n}}a_{n}^{2}\leq a_{n}^{4}.

(5.4)

Set

U_{n}(t)=a_{n}e_{j_{n}},\qquad t\in I_{-}.

(5.5)

The pressure, covariance, vertical residual, and horizontal defect are set to zero:

[P_{n}]=0,\qquad\tau_{n}=0,\qquad\kappa_{n}=0,\qquad G_{n}=0,\qquad\mathcal{V}_{n}=0,\qquad\mu_{h,n}=0.

(5.6)

The only nonzero obstruction is the abstract compatibility residual

g_{n}=\mathcal{C}(U_{n})=\lambda_{j_{n}}a_{n}^{2}e_{j_{n}}.

Thus

\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{Y}=\lambda_{j_{n}}a_{n}^{2}\leq a_{n}^{4}.

(5.7)

Theorem 5.2

(Arbitrary-slow branch in the abstract old trace skeleton)
.

For every sequence
a_{n}\downarrow 0
there exist
\ell_{n}\downarrow 0
,
\delta_{n}\downarrow 0
, and an abstract old trace-skeleton branch of the form (
5.5
)–(
5.6
) such that

(i)

U_{n}\to 0
in the space-time compactness topology;

(ii)

all fixed finite-window compatibility defects vanish eventually;

(iii)

the full compatibility residual satisfies
\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{Y}\leq a_{n}^{4}
, and hence is much smaller than the selected trace scale;

(iv)

covariance, variance, good-time, pressure-gauge, prepared-residual, and horizontal-defect observables are perfectly controlled;

(v)

the selected-time trace distance is

m_{n}=\frac{1}{2}a_{n}^{2}.

(5.8)

Consequently, the abstract trace-obstruction skeleton associated with the old-observable closure permits arbitrary-slow selected-time trace convergence.

Proof.

The size bound is immediate from
\left\|U_{n}(t)\right\|_{H}=a_{n}\to 0
. The pressure-gauge condition is trivial because
[P_{n}]=0
. In the abstract trace model we take the evolution operator to be zero, so
\partial_{t}U_{n}=0
; with
G_{n}=0
and
\tau_{n}=0
, the prepared covariance-form equation is satisfied exactly. This is intentionally weaker than a genuine parabolic equation. The purpose is to test what the abstract old trace skeleton permits before adding parabolic trace-drop and genuine PDE realizability.

Since
\tau_{n}=0
and
\kappa_{n}=0
, the positive covariance and unresolved variance axioms hold. The good-time set and thick good-time set are both all of
I_{-}
. The localized variance balance reduces to
d\kappa_{n}/dt=0
, with
\mu_{h,n}=E_{\mathrm{cut},n}=E_{\delta,n}=0
.

The strict trace class is
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{tr}}=\{0\}
, so

\dist(U_{n}(t),\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{tr}})=\left\|U_{n}(t)\right\|_{H}=a_{n}\to 0.

Thus qualitative compactness holds, but the modulus is the arbitrary sequence
a_{n}
.

For a fixed finite window
\Lambda
, eventually
j_{n}>\Lambda
, and therefore

P_{\Lambda}U_{n}=0,\qquad P_{\Lambda}g_{n}=0.

Hence every fixed finite-window obstruction test sees nothing. In particular, every fixed finite-window trace-cost problem is trivially solvable with zero cost.

Finally, for every
s\in I_{-}
the only strict competitor is
0
. Therefore

\inf_{s\in I_{-}}\inf_{V\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{tr}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left\|U_{n}(s)-V\right\|_{H}^{2}+\kappa_{n}(s)\right]=\frac{1}{2}a_{n}^{2}.

Because
\eta_{n}\leq 2a_{n}^{4}
,

\frac{m_{n}}{\eta_{n}}\geq\frac{1}{4a_{n}^{2}}\to\infty.

Thus the subcritical selection estimate
m_{n}\lesssim\ell_{n}^{\mu}+\ell_{n}^{-N}\delta_{n}^{b}
fails dramatically.
∎

Corollary 5.3

(No universal old-observable rate)
.

No universal logarithmic or power bound for
m_{n}
can be derived from compactness-level projection, fixed finite-window exactifiability, covariance positivity, prepared residual smallness, and good-time variance control alone. The conclusion is an insufficiency statement for the abstract old trace skeleton, not a construction of a Navier–Stokes branch.

Remark 5.4

(What the model violates)
.

The construction violates several stronger structures. First, it violates parabolic trace-drop: the high mode
e_{j_{n}}
remains time-independent instead of being heat-damped. Second, it exploits loss of uniform frequency observability through
\lambda_{j_{n}}\to 0
. Third, if
g_{n}
lies in a phantom cokernel direction, then it violates vertical duality: for
y_{n}=e_{j_{n}}
, one could have
A^{*}y_{n}=0
but

\left\langle g_{n},y_{n}\right\rangle=\lambda_{j_{n}}a_{n}^{2}>0.

A vertical-duality estimate would force this pairing to vanish. Thus vertical duality is precisely the condition forbidding active residuals from living in phantom quotient directions.

6
Obstruction separation: trace-drop, finite windows, and flat branches

The previous model survives because it stores mass in high modes without paying any parabolic price. This section formalizes the first candidate mechanism that kills that naive construction. The underlying intuition is the parabolic smoothing and trace-decay behavior encoded in the heat operator and analytic semigroup theory
[
43
,
44
]
.

6.1
Trace-drop envelope

Let
J_{\rho}
be a low-frequency projection and set
P_{\rho}=I-J_{\rho}
. Consider a failed sharp branch

U_{n}=V_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}W_{n},\qquad\varepsilon_{n}=m_{n}^{1/2},

selected at times
s_{n}
. The following axiom abstracts the high-frequency trace-drop produced by heat smoothing.

Assumption 6.1

(High-frequency trace-drop)
.

There are constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that whenever, for some
\rho>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

\int\phi|P_{\rho}W_{n}(s_{n})|^{2}\,dx\geq\varepsilon_{0},

(6.1)

there exists a measurable set

T_{n,\rho}\subset(s_{n},s_{n}+c_{0}\rho^{2})

with
|T_{n,\rho}|\geq c_{1}\rho^{2}
such that, for
t\in T_{n,\rho}
,

\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|W_{n}(t)|^{2}\,dx\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|W_{n}(s_{n})|^{2}\,dx-c_{1}\varepsilon_{0}+o_{\rho}(1)+o_{n}(1).

(6.2)

Multiplying (
6.2
) by
m_{n}
, one obtains the corresponding selected-trace energy drop:

\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|U_{n}(t)-V_{n}(t)|^{2}\,dx\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|U_{n}(s_{n})-V_{n}(s_{n})|^{2}\,dx-c_{1}\varepsilon_{0}m_{n}+o(m_{n}).

(6.3)

By itself, trace-drop only says that high-frequency energy drops at some future times. To contradict sharp minimality, those drop times must be admissible comparison times and must still have small variance.

Assumption 6.2

(Sharp admissible-time intersection)
.

For every trace-drop set
T_{n,\rho}
arising from Assumption
6.1
, there exists a time
t_{n}\in T_{n,\rho}
such that

(i)

t_{n}
belongs to the admissible sharp comparison set;

(ii)

the same strict shadow
V_{n}
, or an admissible strict competitor with the same normalized first variation, may be used at
t_{n}
;

(iii)

\int\phi\kappa_{n}(t_{n})\,dx=o(m_{n})
.

In concrete arguments, item (iii) is obtained by requiring
s_{n}
to lie in a thick good-time set. If

\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\int_{s_{n}}^{s_{n}+c\rho^{2}}\int\phi\kappa_{n}\,dx\,dt\leq C\ell_{n}^{2}

and
m_{n}\gg\ell_{n}^{\mu}
with
\mu<2
, then
\ell_{n}^{2}=o(m_{n})
, and one can select a time in the drop interval with negligible variance. Assumption
6.2
is the nontrivial statement that such a time also belongs to the admissible comparison set.

6.2
Trace-drop implies trace tightness when admissible times intersect

Proposition 6.3

(Trace-drop plus sharp intersection gives trace tightness)
.

Suppose a failed branch satisfies

m_{n}\gg\eta_{n},\qquad\eta_{n}=\ell_{n}^{\mu}+\ell_{n}^{-N}\delta_{n}^{b},\qquad 0<\mu<1/6,

and satisfies Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
. Then

\lim_{\rho\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int\phi|W_{n}(s_{n})-J_{\rho}W_{n}(s_{n})|^{2}\,dx=0.

(6.4)

Proof.

Assume not. Then there are
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, radii
\rho_{k}\downarrow 0
, and indices
n_{k}\to\infty
such that

\int\phi|P_{\rho_{k}}W_{n_{k}}(s_{n_{k}})|^{2}\,dx\geq\varepsilon_{0}\qquad\text{for every }k.

Fix
k
and apply Assumption
6.1
to the branch index
n_{k}
and the radius
\rho_{k}
. This gives a drop set
T_{n_{k},\rho_{k}}
on which (
6.3
) holds. By Assumption
6.2
, choose
t_{n_{k}}\in T_{n_{k},\rho_{k}}
that is an admissible comparison time and satisfies

\int\phi\kappa_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})\,dx=o(m_{n_{k}}).

Using the same strict competitor, or the admissible competitor supplied by the sharp intersection assumption, at
t_{n_{k}}
, we get

m_{n_{k}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\phi|U_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})-V_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})|^{2}\,dx+\int\phi\kappa_{n_{k}}(t_{n_{k}})\,dx.

The trace-drop estimate gives

m_{n_{k}}\leq m_{n_{k}}-c_{1}\varepsilon_{0}m_{n_{k}}+o(m_{n_{k}}),

which is impossible for large
k
. Therefore the high-frequency tail must vanish in the sense of (
6.4
).
∎

Remark 6.4

(What trace-drop does and does not do)
.

Trace-drop plus admissible-time intersection kills the high-frequency escape model
U_{n}=a_{n}e_{j_{n}}
. It does not by itself prove subcritical selection. Once selected traces are tight, the remaining problem is a finite-window or moving-window singular compatibility problem.

After trace tightness, the next question is whether a fixed finite-dimensional compatibility window can still support arbitrary-slow selected trace convergence. In the analytic case, it cannot.

6.3
Fixed-window Lojasiewicz control

Fix a finite window
\Lambda
. Let

H_{\Lambda}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad Y_{\Lambda}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m},

and let

\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}:H_{\Lambda}\to Y_{\Lambda}

be the finite-window compatibility map. Its zero set is

\mathfrak{S}_{\Lambda}=\{x\in H_{\Lambda}:\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}(x)=0\}.

In the Navier–Stokes shadow problem,
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
is the finite-window projection of the quadratic pressure-compatibility map

\mathcal{C}(V)=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(V_{a}V_{b}).

Thus
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}
is analytic, and in model settings polynomial; this is the finite-dimensional setting in which Łojasiewicz-type analytic inequalities are available
[
42
,
45
,
46
]
.

Proposition 6.5

(Fixed-window analytic obstruction)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}:H_{\Lambda}\to Y_{\Lambda}
be real analytic, and let
K_{\Lambda}\subset H_{\Lambda}
be compact. Then there exist constants
C_{\Lambda}>0
and
\alpha_{\Lambda}>0
such that

\dist(x,\mathfrak{S}_{\Lambda})\leq C_{\Lambda}\left\|\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}(x)\right\|_{Y_{\Lambda}}^{\alpha_{\Lambda}}\qquad\text{for all }x\in K_{\Lambda}.

(6.5)

Consequently, if
x_{n}\in K_{\Lambda}
and
\left\|\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}(x_{n})\right\|_{Y_{\Lambda}}\leq\rho_{n}
, then

\dist(x_{n},\mathfrak{S}_{\Lambda})\leq C_{\Lambda}\rho_{n}^{\alpha_{\Lambda}}.

Proof.

This is the classical finite-dimensional Lojasiewicz inequality for real analytic maps, applied to the analytic function
x\mapsto\left\|\mathcal{C}_{\Lambda}(x)\right\|_{Y_{\Lambda}}^{2}
on a compact neighborhood of its zero set. The constants may depend on
\Lambda
and on
K_{\Lambda}
.
∎

Thus fixed finite-dimensional singularity may worsen the exponent, but it does not permit arbitrary-slow convergence. For example,
\mathcal{C}(x)=x^{k}
gives
\dist(x,\{0\})=|\mathcal{C}(x)|^{1/k}
.

6.4
Why analyticity matters

If the compatibility map were only
C^{\infty}
, arbitrary-slow behavior could occur even in dimension one. Let

\mathcal{C}(x)=\begin{cases}\exp(-1/x^{2}),&x>0,\\
0,&x\leq 0.\end{cases}

Then the zero set is
(-\infty,0]
, and for
x_{n}=a_{n}>0
,

\dist(x_{n},\mathcal{C}^{-1}(0))=a_{n},\qquad\mathcal{C}(x_{n})=\exp(-1/a_{n}^{2}),

which is smaller than every power of
a_{n}
. The strict pressure-compatibility map is quadratic/analytic, so this smooth-flat pathology is not expected in a fixed window.

6.5
Finite-window obstruction dichotomy

Let a trace-tight failed branch be expanded formally as

V^{\eta}=V+\eta R_{1}+\eta^{2}R_{2}+\eta^{3}R_{3}+\cdots.

For the quadratic compatibility form

B(A,B)=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(A_{a}B_{b}),

the projected obstruction hierarchy in a fixed window is

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{2,\Lambda}

\displaystyle=P_{\Lambda}B(R_{1},R_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{3,\Lambda}

\displaystyle=P_{\Lambda}\bigl(2B(R_{1},R_{2})\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{4,\Lambda}

\displaystyle=P_{\Lambda}\bigl(2B(R_{1},R_{3})+B(R_{2},R_{2})\bigr),

and generally

\mathcal{O}_{k,\Lambda}=P_{\Lambda}\sum_{i+j=k}B(R_{i},R_{j}).

(6.6)

If some
\mathcal{O}_{k,\Lambda}\neq 0
, then a finite-order visible obstruction appears and the fixed-window analytic mechanism yields a finite-power improvement. If all
\mathcal{O}_{k,\Lambda}=0
for every fixed
\Lambda
and every finite
k
, the branch is finite-mode flat to all orders. The remaining obstruction is no longer ordinary finite-dimensional singularity; it is all-order flatness or moving-window exactification.

Trace-drop removes high-frequency escape, and fixed-window analyticity removes ordinary finite-dimensional no-rate behavior. Yet one more abstract obstruction remains: every finite stage may be exactifiable, but the finite-stage constants may have no summable majorant.

6.6
A non-summable finite-stage model

Let

H=\mathbb{R}e_{1}\oplus\ell^{2}(\{e_{2},e_{3},\ldots\}),

and write

x=(a,z_{2},z_{3},\ldots).

Choose positive constants
\Gamma_{k}\to\infty
growing very fast, for example
\Gamma_{k}=\exp(k^{2})
. For each finite stage
K
, define

\mathfrak{S}_{K}=\{(a,z_{2},\ldots,z_{K}):z_{k}=\Gamma_{k}a^{k},\ 2\leq k\leq K\}.

(6.7)

The full strict set is the set of all
(a,z_{2},z_{3},\ldots)\in H
satisfying

z_{k}=\Gamma_{k}a^{k},\qquad k\geq 2.

(6.8)

If
\Gamma_{k}=\exp(k^{2})
, then for every
a\neq 0
,

\sum_{k\geq 2}\Gamma_{k}^{2}|a|^{2k}=\infty.

Thus the full strict set is only

\mathfrak{S}=\{0\}.

Nevertheless, every finite-stage strict set
\mathfrak{S}_{K}
is nontrivial.

Theorem 6.6

(All-order finite-stage exactification does not imply global selection)
.

For any sequence
a_{n}\downarrow 0
, the branch

U_{n}=a_{n}e_{1}

is trace-tight and has selected trace distance

m_{n}=\frac{1}{2}a_{n}^{2}

from the full strict set
\mathfrak{S}=\{0\}
. However, for every fixed finite stage
K
, there exists
\widetilde{U}_{n,K}\in\mathfrak{S}_{K}
such that

\left\|\widetilde{U}_{n,K}-U_{n}\right\|_{H}=o(a_{n}).

Thus every fixed finite stage declares the branch exactifiable to a lower order than the main trace distance, while the full strict curve does not exist for any
a\neq 0
.

Proof.

The branch is contained in the one-dimensional span of
e_{1}
, so trace tightness is immediate. Since the full strict set is
\{0\}
,

\dist(U_{n},\mathfrak{S})=a_{n},\qquad m_{n}=\frac{1}{2}a_{n}^{2}.

For fixed
K
, define

\widetilde{U}_{n,K}=(a_{n},\Gamma_{2}a_{n}^{2},\Gamma_{3}a_{n}^{3},\ldots,\Gamma_{K}a_{n}^{K},0,0,\ldots).

Then
\widetilde{U}_{n,K}\in\mathfrak{S}_{K}
, and

\left\|\widetilde{U}_{n,K}-U_{n}\right\|_{H}^{2}=\sum_{k=2}^{K}\Gamma_{k}^{2}a_{n}^{2k}.

For fixed
K
, each term is
o(a_{n}^{2})
, hence the whole sum is
o(a_{n}^{2})
. This proves the claim.
∎

Remark 6.7

(Meaning of the model)
.

The constants
\Gamma_{k}
represent finite-stage right-inverse, Newton, or trace-lifting constants, as in finite-dimensional bifurcation/Newton schemes
[
47
]
. The theorem shows that the existence of all finite jets does not imply an actual strict curve unless one has a summable majorant or a different mechanism, such as trace-cost exactification or vertical duality. It is an abstract model of the finite-mode flat surviving branch.

7
Trace-cost duality and active residual quotients

The preceding all-order model shows that strong all-frequency exactification may be too much to ask. Trace-cost duality is the weaker, selected-time substitute.

7.1
Finite-dimensional trace cost

Let
H
and
Y
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and let

A:H\to Y

be linear. For
g\in Y
, define

\Cost_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)=\inf\{\left\|\xi\right\|_{H}^{2}:A\xi=-g\},

(7.1)

with the convention
\Cost_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)=+\infty
if
-g\notin\Range A
.

Lemma 7.1

(Trace-cost duality)
.

For
g\in Y
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\Cost_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)\leq r^{2}
;

(ii)

for every
y\in Y
,

|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle_{Y}|\leq r\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}.

(7.2)

Equivalently,

\sqrt{\Cost_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)}=\sup_{A^{*}y\neq 0}\frac{|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle_{Y}|}{\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}},

(7.3)

with the value
+\infty
if
g\notin\Range A
.

Proof.

If
A\xi=-g
, then

|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle_{Y}|=|\left\langle-A\xi,y\right\rangle_{Y}|=|\left\langle\xi,A^{*}y\right\rangle_{H}|\leq\left\|\xi\right\|_{H}\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}.

Taking the infimum over admissible
\xi
gives one implication. Conversely, if (
7.2
) holds, then
\left\langle g,y\right\rangle=0
for all
y\in\Ker A^{*}
, so
g\in(\Ker A^{*})^{\perp}=\Range A
. Let
\xi_{0}
be the minimal-norm solution of
A\xi=-g
. Since
\xi_{0}\in\Range A^{*}
, choose
y_{0}
with
A^{*}y_{0}=\xi_{0}
. Then

\left\|\xi_{0}\right\|_{H}^{2}=|\left\langle\xi_{0},A^{*}y_{0}\right\rangle_{H}|=|\left\langle-A\xi_{0},y_{0}\right\rangle_{Y}|=|\left\langle g,y_{0}\right\rangle_{Y}|\leq r\left\|A^{*}y_{0}\right\|_{H}=r\left\|\xi_{0}\right\|_{H}.

Thus
\left\|\xi_{0}\right\|_{H}\leq r
, and
\Cost_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}(g)\leq r^{2}
. The equality follows by optimizing over
r
.
∎

7.2
Vertical-duality as anti-phantom quotient control

For a finite window, an active residual
g_{\Lambda}\in Y_{\Lambda}
defines an obstruction class

[g_{\Lambda}]\in Y_{\Lambda}/\Range A_{\Lambda}.

The trace cost is finite exactly when this class vanishes, and it is small exactly when
g_{\Lambda}
has a small preimage under
A_{\Lambda}
. By Lemma
7.1
, this is equivalent to

|\left\langle g_{\Lambda},y\right\rangle|\leq r_{\Lambda}\left\|A_{\Lambda}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{\Lambda}}.

(7.4)

Thus small singular values of
A_{\Lambda}
are harmless if the actual residual has proportionally small pairing with the corresponding left singular directions.

Assumption 7.2

(Vertical-duality active-residual estimate)
.

For every finite-window failed-selection branch and every active residual
g_{a,n}(\eta)\in Y_{a,n,\eta}
, there is a branch-native scale

r_{a,n}(\eta)=C_{a}\eta^{K+1}+C_{a}\rho_{n}\varepsilon_{n}^{-p_{a}}\eta

(7.5)

such that

|\left\langle g_{a,n}(\eta),y\right\rangle|\leq r_{a,n}(\eta)\left\|A_{a,n,\eta}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{a,n,\eta}}

(7.6)

for every
y\in Y_{a,n,\eta}
.

By Lemma
7.1
, Assumption
7.2
yields

\Cost^{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_{a,n,\eta}}(g_{a,n}(\eta))\leq r_{a,n}(\eta)^{2}.

Hence vertical duality provides exactly the selected-time correction that the all-order non-summable model lacks.

8
Navier–Stokes realizability and vertical lift cost

This section starts the Navier–Stokes-specific part of the paper. The old-envelope no-rate model and the finite-stage flat model are larger than the true Navier–Stokes image. To decide whether the surviving branch is relevant to Navier–Stokes, one must ask whether the horizontal formal branch can be lifted to a full three-dimensional deformation with a small vertical component.

8.1
The Navier–Stokes deformation complex

Let
(V,Q)
be a strict shadow:

V=(V_{h},0),\qquad\nabla_{h}\cdot V_{h}=0,\qquad\partial_{3}Q=0,

\partial_{t}V_{h}-\Delta V_{h}+\nabla_{h}\cdot(V_{h}\otimes V_{h})+\nabla_{h}Q=0.

Consider a formal full Navier–Stokes deformation

\displaystyle u_{h}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=V_{h}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon^{k}r_{k},

(8.1)

\displaystyle u_{3}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon^{k}z_{k},

(8.2)

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=Q+\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon^{k}\pi_{k}.

(8.3)

The incompressibility condition gives, at each order,

\nabla_{h}\cdot r_{k}+\partial_{3}z_{k}=0.

(8.4)

Thus a horizontal strict jet with nonzero horizontal divergence must be compensated by a vertical jet. If the required
z_{k}
is large in the vertical budget norm, the horizontal branch is not realizable by a small-
u_{3}
degeneration.

Define

L_{V}z=\partial_{t}z-\Delta z+V_{h}\cdot\nabla_{h}z,

and

L_{V}^{h}r=\partial_{t}r-\Delta r+V_{h}\cdot\nabla_{h}r+r\cdot\nabla_{h}V_{h}.

The vertical momentum equation gives

L_{V}z_{k}+\partial_{3}\pi_{k}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl(r_{i}\cdot\nabla_{h}z_{j}+z_{i}\partial_{3}z_{j}\bigr).

(8.5)

The horizontal momentum equation gives

L_{V}^{h}r_{k}+\nabla_{h}\pi_{k}+z_{k}\partial_{3}V_{h}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\bigl(r_{i}\cdot\nabla_{h}r_{j}+z_{i}\partial_{3}r_{j}\bigr).

(8.6)

The strict-shadow hierarchy is the special slice in which
z_{k}=0
,
\partial_{3}\pi_{k}=0
, and
\nabla_{h}\cdot r_{k}=0
. A formal strict branch may therefore be larger than the class of branches realizable by full Navier–Stokes deformations.

8.2
Vertical lift cost

The strict compatibility obstruction at order
k
is a horizontal pressure obstruction. In the full three-dimensional system, it need not vanish; it may instead be absorbed by
z_{k}
through (
8.5
). This motivates the following definition.

Definition 8.1

(Finite-order vertical lift cost)
.

Fix a strict base
(V,Q)
and horizontal jets
\mathcal{R}_{\leq k}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
. The order-
k
vertical lift cost is

\mathrm{VCost}_{V,k}(\mathcal{R}_{\leq k})=\inf\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left\|z_{j}\right\|_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\alpha_{j}},

(8.7)

where the infimum is taken over vertical jets
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
and pressure jets
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
satisfying (
8.4
), (
8.5
), and (
8.6
) for
1\leq j\leq k
. Here
\mathcal{Z}
is a vertical budget norm compatible with the one-component quantity
C_{3}
.

If
\mathrm{VCost}_{V,k}=+\infty
, the horizontal jets cannot be realized even formally to order
k
. If the costs grow too fast to admit a summable majorant, then all finite jets may exist without a genuine small-vertical-component family.

Definition 8.2

(NS-realizable horizontal jet branch)
.

An infinite horizontal jet branch
(r_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is
\mathrm{NS}
-realizable with vertical budget
\delta
at amplitude
\varepsilon
if there exist vertical jets
(z_{k})_{k\geq 1}
and pressure jets
(\pi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
solving the jet equations to all orders such that

\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon^{k}\left\|z_{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{Z}}<\infty

and the resulting vertical component

u_{3}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varepsilon^{k}z_{k}

satisfies

\left\|u_{3}^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{3}}^{3}\leq\delta.

8.3
Zero-shadow quadratic calculation

The first nontrivial test occurs at second order. Let
V=0
,
Q=0
, and let the first horizontal jet be
r_{1}=W
. For a strict first tangent one has

\nabla_{h}\cdot W=0,\qquad z_{1}=0,\qquad\partial_{3}\pi_{1}=0.

At second order, the pressure generated by the horizontal quadratic interaction satisfies formally

-\Delta\pi_{2}=\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(W_{a}W_{b})

(8.8)

(up to the chosen pressure gauge and lower-order corrections). The strict 2.5D condition would require
\partial_{3}\pi_{2}=0
, equivalently the quadratic strict obstruction

B(W,W)=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}\Delta_{h}^{-1}\partial_{a}\partial_{b}(W_{a}W_{b})

to vanish in the relevant quotient. Full Navier–Stokes allows instead

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)z_{2}+\partial_{3}\pi_{2}=0.

(8.9)

Thus the quadratic strict obstruction is converted into the cost of solving (
8.9
) with small
z_{2}
.

A model second-order vertical cost is therefore

\mathrm{VCost}_{2}(W)=\inf\{\left\|z_{2}\right\|_{\mathcal{Z}}:(\partial_{t}-\Delta)z_{2}=-\partial_{3}\pi_{2}[W,W]\}.

(8.10)

If
B(W,W)\neq 0
and
\mathrm{VCost}_{2}(W)
is bounded below at the sharp scale, then the direction cannot occur in a degeneration with sufficiently small
u_{3}
. If
B(W,W)=0
, the obstruction must be sought at higher order.

8.4
Symbol-level conclusion

In the periodic zero-shadow test, the strict quadratic obstruction and the full vertical lift have different symbols. For a horizontal frequency
K
, vertical frequency
m
, and horizontal trace normalized by
\|W\|_{L^{2}}\sim 1
, the strict obstruction has size

\|B(W,W)\|_{L^{2}}\sim mK,

whereas the canonical second vertical lift satisfies

\|z_{2}\|_{L^{2}}\sim\frac{mK^{2}}{(K^{2}+m^{2})^{2}}.

Thus a strict pressure obstruction is not automatically an NS-realizability obstruction. It becomes one only if the associated vertical lift cost is too large for the available
C_{3}
-budget. The detailed Fourier computation is given in
Appendix

A
.

9
Reduced vertical duality and Schur trace-projectability

The preceding section shows that raw strict pressure obstruction and full vertical lift cost are not identical. This section formulates the correct reduced target. Vertical duality is not a raw factorization on all quotient directions; it is a residual-level anti-phantom statement after all finite-order visible obstructions have been removed.

9.1
A periodic zero-shadow finite-window factorization test

The preceding discussion also identifies a trap. One should not require vertical-duality factorization on the raw zero-shadow second-order quotient. In that raw quotient the trace-defect map is zero, while the vertical adjoint lifting is generally nonzero.

Work again on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
in a fixed finite Fourier window and at the zero strict base
(V,Q)=(0,0)
. The raw second-order strict quotient is generated by

g=B(W,W)=\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}(-\Delta_{h})^{-1}F(W).

For
y\in Y_{\mathrm{raw}}
, the strict-to-vertical adjoint lifting is, on active modes,

\widehat{B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}y}(\xi)=\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{|\xi_{h}|^{2}}\,i\xi_{h}\cdot\widehat{y}(\xi),\qquad\xi_{h}\neq 0.

(9.1)

On modes with
\xi_{3}=0
, the vertical obstruction is absent and one may set
B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}y=0
.

By contrast, the raw trace-defect map at the zero shadow is

A_{\mathrm{raw}}=D\mathcal{C}_{0}.

Since
\mathcal{C}(V)=B(V,V)
is quadratic,

A_{\mathrm{raw}}=D\mathcal{C}_{0}=0,\qquad A_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}=0.

(9.2)

Therefore an exact raw factorization

B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}=MA_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}

would force
B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}=0
, which is false in general by (
9.1
). We record the conclusion explicitly.

Proposition 9.1

(Raw zero-shadow factorization obstruction)
.

In the periodic zero-shadow finite-window model, the raw second-order trace-defect map satisfies
A_{\mathrm{raw}}=0
, while the strict-to-vertical adjoint lifting
B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}
is generally nonzero and has Fourier symbol (
9.1
). Hence no exact factorization

B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}=MA_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}

can hold on the raw second-order quotient.

Proof.

The identity
A_{\mathrm{raw}}=0
follows from the quadratic nature of
\mathcal{C}(V)=B(V,V)
, hence
D\mathcal{C}_{0}=0
. Formula (
9.1
) follows from the equality

\left\langle\nabla_{h}\partial_{3}(-\Delta_{h})^{-1}F,y\right\rangle=\left\langle\partial_{3}(-\Delta)^{-1}F,B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}y\right\rangle

on active Fourier modes. Choosing a dual vector
y
with
i\xi_{h}\cdot\widehat{y}(\xi)\neq 0
on an active mode gives
B_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}y\neq 0
. Since
A_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}=0
, no factorization through
A_{\mathrm{raw}}^{*}
can hold on the raw quotient.
∎

This failure is not a failure of the vertical-duality route. It says that raw second-order zero-shadow obstructions are visible finite-order obstructions. They should be handled by the finite-order obstruction and Lojasiewicz selection mechanism, not by vertical duality. Vertical duality is meant only for the genuinely surviving active quotient after all visible finite-order obstruction directions have been removed.

We therefore refine the quotient before asking for any vertical-duality statement.

9.2
Reduced active quotients after finite-order obstruction removal

Let
(V,Q)\in\mathfrak{S}_{M}
be a strict base and let

V_{\eta}^{(K)}=V+\eta R_{1}+\eta^{2}R_{2}+\cdots+\eta^{K}R_{K}

be a finite-stage formal strict branch. Expanding the strict compatibility map gives

\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta}^{(K)})=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\eta^{j}\mathcal{O}_{j}+\eta^{K+1}\mathcal{R}_{K+1}(\eta).

(9.3)

For a zero strict base,
\mathcal{C}(V)=B(V,V)
, so

\mathcal{O}_{1}=0,\qquad\mathcal{O}_{2}=B(R_{1},R_{1}),

\mathcal{O}_{3}=B(R_{1},R_{2})+B(R_{2},R_{1}),

and, in general,

\mathcal{O}_{k}=\sum_{i+j=k}B(R_{i},R_{j}).

(9.4)

At a nonzero base the same construction applies, but the first coefficient is

D\mathcal{C}_{V}[R_{1}]
and the later coefficients contain the corresponding moving-base lower-order terms.

Fix a finite window
\Lambda
, and let
Y_{\Lambda}
be the active compatibility quotient. Write

\mathcal{O}_{j,\Lambda}=P_{\Lambda}\mathcal{O}_{j}\in Y_{\Lambda}.

The visible obstruction subspace up to order
K
is

Y_{\mathrm{vis}}^{(K)}:=\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{O}_{1,\Lambda},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{K,\Lambda}\}\subset Y_{\Lambda}.

(9.5)

These are precisely the directions that have already appeared at finite order. If one of them is nonzero, it is a finite-order visible obstruction and should be handled by finite-dimensional analytic geometry and the selection argument. It should not be assigned to vertical duality.

The reduced active quotient is

Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}:=Y_{\Lambda}/Y_{\mathrm{vis}}^{(K)},\qquad\Pi_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}:Y_{\Lambda}\to Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}.

(9.6)

Equivalently, its dual is the annihilator

(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}=\{y\in Y_{\Lambda}^{*}:\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{j,\Lambda},y\right\rangle=0,\ 1\leq j\leq K\}.

(9.7)

Thus, for every
y\in(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}
, the low-order pairings vanish and

\left\langle\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta}^{(K)}),y\right\rangle=\eta^{K+1}\left\langle\mathcal{R}_{K+1}(\eta),y\right\rangle.

(9.8)

This is the algebraic reason why vertical duality is meaningful only after finite-order obstruction directions have been removed.

We define the reduced branch-native residual by

g_{K}(\eta):=\Pi_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}\left[\eta^{-(K+1)}\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta}^{(K)})\right]\in Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}.

(9.9)

This is the surviving active residual. It is not a raw obstruction coefficient; it is what remains after quotienting out the visible finite-order directions.

We also define the reduced trace-defect map. Let
H_{K}
be the selected-time trace correction space, and let

\mathcal{L}_{K}:H_{K}\to X_{\Lambda}

be a finite-stage trace-lifting map. For
\xi\in H_{K}
, set
h=\mathcal{L}_{K}\xi
and consider

V_{\eta,\xi}^{(K)}=V_{\eta}^{(K)}+\eta^{K+1}h.

Then

A_{K}\xi:=\Pi_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}D\mathcal{C}_{V_{\eta}^{(K)}}[\mathcal{L}_{K}\xi]\in Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}.

(9.10)

The adjoint

A_{K}^{*}:(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}\to H_{K}

measures which reduced dual quotient directions are visible at the selected trace. If
A_{K}^{*}y=0
, then
y
is a surviving trace-invisible dual direction.

The vertical adjoint lifting on the reduced quotient is a map

B_{K}^{*}:(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}\to\mathcal{V}_{K}

chosen so that the reduced residual pairing has the representation

\left\langle g_{K}(\eta),y\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{NS},K}(\eta),B_{K}^{*}y\right\rangle+\operatorname{Comm}_{K}(y).

(9.11)

Here
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{NS},K}(\eta)
is the
K
-stage vertical momentum residual generated by the Navier–Stokes branch, while
\operatorname{Comm}_{K}
collects cutoff, projection, moving-base, and gauge remainders.

The correct reduced vertical-duality target is therefore

|\left\langle g_{K}(\eta),y\right\rangle|\leq r_{K}(\eta)\left\|A_{K}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{K}}\qquad\forall y\in(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}.

(9.12)

A sufficient operator-level statement is a finite-stage factorization

B_{K}^{*}y=M_{K}A_{K}^{*}y+R_{K}^{*}y,

(9.13)

with

\left|\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{NS},K}(\eta),R_{K}^{*}y\right\rangle\right|+|\operatorname{Comm}_{K}(y)|\leq r_{K}(\eta)\left\|A_{K}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{K}},

and
\left\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{NS},K}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathcal{V}_{K}^{\prime}}\leq r_{K}(\eta)
. Then (
9.11
) implies (
9.12
). By the trace-cost lemma, (
9.12
) gives

g_{K}(\eta)\in\Range A_{K},\qquad\Cost^{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{K}(\eta))\leq Cr_{K}(\eta)^{2}.

(9.14)

Thus the surviving active residual can be removed by a selected-time correction of small trace cost.

9.3
Recursive obstruction removal and reduced vertical duality

The preceding construction yields a general finite-stage scheme. Let
V_{\eta}^{(K)}
be a
K
-stage formal branch and let
\mathcal{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{K}
be the obstruction coefficients in (
9.3
). At each stage exactly one of the following alternatives occurs.

Proposition 9.2

(Recursive obstruction-removal alternative)
.

Fix a finite window
\Lambda
and a finite stage
K
. For the branch
V_{\eta}^{(K)}
, either:

(i)

there exists
1\leq j\leq K
such that
\mathcal{O}_{j,\Lambda}\neq 0
in the active quotient; in this case a finite-order visible obstruction is present and finite-dimensional analytic control gives a finite-power selection improvement at this stage; or

(ii)

all visible obstruction coefficients up to order
K
vanish in the active quotient, and the only object left for trace-cost analysis is the reduced residual
g_{K}(\eta)\in Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}
.

In the second case, if the reduced VD estimate (
9.12
) holds, then
g_{K}(\eta)
admits a trace correction with cost bounded by
Cr_{K}(\eta)^{2}
.

Proof.

The alternative is tautological from the definition of the span
Y_{\mathrm{vis}}^{(K)}
. If a nonzero
\mathcal{O}_{j,\Lambda}
appears, then a finite-dimensional analytic Lojasiewicz inequality on the fixed window gives a finite-power relation between compatibility residual and distance to the finite-window zero set. This is precisely the finite-order obstruction branch. If no such coefficient appears, then the annihilator formula (
9.7
) gives (
9.8
), and the surviving residual is exactly
g_{K}
. Finally, the trace-cost consequence follows from the finite-dimensional duality lemma applied to
A_{K}:H_{K}\to Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}
.
∎

Passing
K\to\infty
, a genuinely surviving finite-mode flat branch is one for which

\mathcal{O}_{j}=0\qquad\text{for every finite }j

in every fixed active window. Then either the formal strict series converges and gives an exact strict curve, or the formal series is non-summable and one must use finite-stage trace-cost exactification. Reduced vertical duality is the mechanism that makes this finite-stage exactification possible without a global all-frequency inverse.

9.4
Finite-window residual anti-phantom criterion

There is one final correction to the formulation. The operator-level factorization
B_{K}^{*}=M_{K}A_{K}^{*}
is generally too strong. The exact finite-dimensional condition needed for vertical duality is a residual-level range inclusion.

Let
H,Y,\mathcal{V}
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, with

A:H\to Y,\qquad B^{*}:Y^{*}\to\mathcal{V}.

Let
B:\mathcal{V}^{*}\to Y
denote the adjoint map, so that

\left\langle B\mathcal{V},y\right\rangle_{Y}=\left\langle\mathcal{V},B^{*}y\right\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}}.

In the clean periodic model, the residual representation has no commutator, and hence

g=B\mathcal{V}.

(9.15)

The reduced VD inequality

|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle|\leq r\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}\qquad\forall y\in Y^{*}

(9.16)

is equivalent to

g\in\Range A\qquad\text{and}\qquad\inf_{A\xi=g}\left\|\xi\right\|_{H}\leq r.

(9.17)

Indeed,
(\Range A)^{\perp}=\Ker A^{*}
, so (
9.16
) first says that
g
has no component in
\Ker A^{*}
, and then bounds the minimal preimage.

Proposition 9.3

(Finite-window residual anti-phantom criterion)
.

Assume the clean finite-dimensional residual representation
g=B\mathcal{V}
. Then (
9.16
) holds if and only if

B\mathcal{V}\in\Range A

(9.18)

and

\left\|A^{\dagger}B\mathcal{V}\right\|_{H}\leq r,

(9.19)

where
A^{\dagger}
is the minimal-norm right inverse on
\Range A
. Equivalently,

P_{\Ker A^{*}}B\mathcal{V}=0

and the minimal preimage is bounded by
r
.

Proof.

If (
9.16
) holds and
y\in\Ker A^{*}
, then
\left\langle g,y\right\rangle=0
. Hence
g\perp\Ker A^{*}=(\Range A)^{\perp}
, so
g\in\Range A
. The inequality then gives the operator norm of the linear functional
A^{*}y\mapsto\left\langle g,y\right\rangle
, which is exactly the minimal-norm preimage bound. Conversely, if
g=A\xi
with
\left\|\xi\right\|_{H}\leq r
, then

|\left\langle g,y\right\rangle|=|\left\langle\xi,A^{*}y\right\rangle|\leq r\left\|A^{*}y\right\|_{H}.

Taking the minimal such
\xi
gives (
9.19
).
∎

Thus vertical duality is not an automatic property of the strict quotient geometry. It is a property of the Navier–Stokes-derived residual. Strong operator factorization would say

\Range B\subseteq\Range A

for all possible vertical residuals. This is usually too strong. What is needed is only

B_{K}\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{NS},K}(\eta)\in\Range A_{K}

(9.20)

with a small minimal preimage. In dual language,

P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}g_{K}(\eta)=0.

(9.21)

The updated theorem target is therefore the following range-inclusion form of reduced vertical duality.

Problem 9.4

(NS-derived residual range inclusion)
.

For every finite stage
K
, finite window
\Lambda
, and
\mathrm{NS}
-derived surviving branch, prove

g_{K}(\eta)\in\Range A_{K}

and

\inf_{A_{K}\xi=-g_{K}(\eta)}\left\|\xi\right\|_{H_{K}}\leq r_{K}(\eta).

Equivalently, prove

|\left\langle g_{K}(\eta),y\right\rangle|\leq r_{K}(\eta)\left\|A_{K}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{K}}\qquad\forall y\in(Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)})^{*}.

This is reduced vertical duality stated as a residual range-inclusion theorem rather than as an overly strong operator factorization theorem.

The natural primal explanation is finite-stage
\mathrm{NS}
-realizability. If the branch admits a full Navier–Stokes deformation tail

u^{\eta}=V_{\eta}^{(K)}+\eta^{K+1}h_{\mathrm{tail}}+\cdots

whose equation is exact to order
K+1
, then the selected trace

\xi_{\mathrm{tail}}=h_{\mathrm{tail}}(s_{*})

should satisfy

A_{K}\xi_{\mathrm{tail}}=-g_{K}(\eta).

This would imply
g_{K}(\eta)\in\Range A_{K}
directly. In this sense, residual-level vertical duality is the finite-stage shadow of full Navier–Stokes realizability.

9.5
Vertical trace-projectability from finite-stage NS realizability

The range-inclusion formulation clarifies one further point. Finite-stage Navier–Stokes realizability does not, by itself, immediately imply

g_{K}(\eta)\in\Range A_{K}.

It gives a larger balance involving both a horizontal trace correction and a vertical lift. This distinction is important, because the strict trace-cost method can only use the horizontal trace-defect range, whereas a full three-dimensional Navier–Stokes correction also contains a vertical component.

Fix a finite stage and finite window, and write

Y_{K}=Y_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)},\qquad H_{K}=\text{selected-time trace correction space},\qquad Z_{K}=\text{vertical lift space}.

The reduced active residual is

g_{K}(\eta)=\Pi_{\mathrm{red}}^{(K)}\eta^{-(K+1)}\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta}^{(K)})\in Y_{K},

and the strict trace-defect map is

A_{K}:H_{K}\to Y_{K}.

A full Navier–Stokes finite-stage tail also contains a vertical variable

z\in Z_{K}.

Its contribution to the reduced strict quotient will be denoted by

B_{K}:Z_{K}\to Y_{K}.

Thus the finite-stage full Navier–Stokes balance has the schematic form

g_{K}+A_{K}\xi+B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K}(\xi,z)=0,

(9.22)

where
\mathcal{N}_{K}
denotes higher-order or branch-native truncation terms.

Equation (
9.22
) does not imply
g_{K}\in\Range A_{K}
unless the vertical contribution is itself trace-projectable. Let

P_{K}^{\perp}:Y_{K}\to(\Range A_{K})^{\perp}=\Ker A_{K}^{*}

be the phantom projection. Projecting (
9.22
) gives

P_{K}^{\perp}g_{K}+P_{K}^{\perp}B_{K}z+P_{K}^{\perp}\mathcal{N}_{K}(\xi,z)=0.

Since
P_{K}^{\perp}A_{K}\xi=0
, strict trace corrections cannot affect this equation. Therefore the actual obstruction is the vertical trace-projectability defect

\mathfrak{p}_{K}(z):=P_{K}^{\perp}B_{K}z\in(\Range A_{K})^{\perp}.

(9.23)

If
\mathfrak{p}_{K}(z)\neq 0
, the vertical lift creates a strict quotient component invisible to selected-time trace correction.

Proposition 9.5

(Trace-projectable NS realizability implies residual range inclusion)
.

Assume that there exist
\xi\in H_{K}
,
z\in Z_{K}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{K}\in Y_{K}
such that

g_{K}+A_{K}\xi+B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K}=0.

Assume further that

B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K}\in\Range A_{K}.

Then
g_{K}\in\Range A_{K}
. More precisely, if
A_{K}\zeta=B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K}
, then

g_{K}=-A_{K}(\xi+\zeta),

and hence

\Cost^{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{K})\leq\left\|\xi+\zeta\right\|_{H_{K}}^{2}.

Proof.

The proof is immediate from the balance equation. Substituting
B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K}=A_{K}\zeta
gives

g_{K}+A_{K}\xi+A_{K}\zeta=0.

Thus
g_{K}=-A_{K}(\xi+\zeta)
, and
-(\xi+\zeta)
is an admissible trace preimage of
g_{K}
. Taking its squared norm gives the trace-cost bound.
∎

The dual version is the reduced vertical-duality inequality

|\left\langle B_{K}z+\mathcal{N}_{K},y\right\rangle|\leq r_{K}(\eta)\left\|A_{K}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{K}}\qquad\forall y\in Y_{K}^{*}.

(9.24)

Thus the genuinely missing mechanism is not merely the existence of a vertical lift. It is the assertion that the vertical lift can be traded for a strict selected-time trace correction at small trace cost. We call this
vertical trace-projectability
.

Problem 9.6

(Vertical trace-projectability of NS-realizable tails)
.

For every finite window and every finite stage, prove that the finite-stage vertical lift contribution of an
\mathrm{NS}
-derived surviving branch satisfies

B_{K}z_{K}+\mathcal{N}_{K}\in\Range A_{K}

up to an error whose trace cost is bounded by the branch-native scale. Equivalently, prove the dual estimate (
9.24
).

9.6
Periodic finite-window trace-projectability and the Schur phantom map

The preceding discussion gives a concrete finite-dimensional test. In a clean periodic finite-window model all spaces are finite-dimensional. Let

A:H\to Y,\qquad B:Z\to Y,

where
H
is the trace correction space,
Z
is the vertical lift space, and
Y
is the reduced active quotient. The vertical trace-projectability condition is

B(Z^{\mathrm{NS}})\subseteq\Range A,

where
Z^{\mathrm{NS}}\subseteq Z
is the subspace of vertical responses that actually arise from finite-stage Navier–Stokes forcing.

In a periodic zero-shadow Stokes symbol calculation, the homogeneous tail system

i\xi_{h}\cdot h+i\xi_{3}z=0,

(i\omega+|\xi|^{2})z+i\xi_{3}q=0,\qquad(i\omega+|\xi|^{2})h+i\xi_{h}q=0

has no nonzero solution for
|\xi|\neq 0
. Thus the relevant vertical space is not a homogeneous kernel. It is the image of the lower-order forcing through the full Stokes response. We write this as

z=C_{K}\beta,

where
\beta
denotes the finite-stage forcing parameters and

C_{K}:\mathcal{F}_{K}\to Z_{K}

is the finite-window vertical Stokes response operator.

Consequently the object to test is the finite-dimensional Schur phantom map

\mathfrak{S}_{K}:=P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}B_{K}C_{K}:\mathcal{F}_{K}\to(\Range A_{K})^{\perp}.

(9.25)

Here
P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}
is the orthogonal projection onto the phantom cokernel.

Proposition 9.7

(Schur range criterion and trace-cost bound)
.

In the periodic finite-window reduced model, the Schur cancellation condition

\mathfrak{S}_{K}\beta=0

for every
\mathrm{NS}
-derived forcing parameter
\beta
is equivalent to the
range-inclusion
part of vertical trace-projectability:

B_{K}C_{K}\beta\in\Range A_{K}.

Equivalently,

P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}B_{K}C_{K}\beta=0.

The full reduced vertical-duality estimate additionally requires a quantitative right-inverse bound:

\left\|A_{K}^{\dagger}B_{K}C_{K}\beta\right\|_{H_{K}}\leq r_{K}(\eta),

up to the branch-native error scale. Equivalently, the complete trace-cost statement is

|\left\langle B_{K}C_{K}\beta,y\right\rangle|\leq r_{K}(\eta)\left\|A_{K}^{*}y\right\|_{H_{K}}\qquad\forall y\in Y_{K}^{*}.

Thus Schur cancellation removes the phantom projection, while reduced vertical duality also controls the minimal trace preimage.

This reformulation is useful because
A_{K}
,
B_{K}
, and
C_{K}
are matrices in a fixed finite Fourier window. The vague phrase “prove VD” is thus reduced to two concrete tasks: computing or annihilating the Schur map (
9.25
), and proving the corresponding Moore–Penrose right-inverse bound on the NS-derived residual.

9.7
Does finite-mode flatness force Schur cancellation?

The preceding calculation raises a natural question. If a branch is already finite-mode flat in the strict compatibility hierarchy, must it satisfy the Schur cancellation condition

P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}B_{K}C_{K}F_{K}=0?

In general the answer is no without additional structure.

Strict finite-mode flatness controls the coefficients in the strict compatibility expansion

\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta})=\sum_{j\geq 1}\eta^{j}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}},

where, in the zero-shadow quadratic case,

\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\mathrm{str}}=\sum_{i+j=k}B(R_{i},R_{j}).

(9.26)

It requires

P_{\Lambda}\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\mathrm{str}}=0\qquad\text{for every fixed }\Lambda\text{ and every }k.

Schur cancellation controls a different object:

\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\mathrm{Schur}}:=P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}B_{K}C_{K}F_{K}.

(9.27)

This object measures whether the full Navier–Stokes vertical response is trace-projectable in the reduced strict quotient. It is a range-alignment condition, not merely the vanishing of a strict compatibility coefficient.

A simple finite-dimensional example makes the logical independence clear. Let

Y=\mathbb{R}^{2},\qquad H=\mathbb{R},\qquad Ah=(h,0).

Then
\Range A=\operatorname{span}\{(1,0)\}
and
\Ker A^{*}=\operatorname{span}\{(0,1)\}
. Suppose all strict obstruction coefficients vanish:

\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}}=0\qquad\forall j.

But take a vertical Schur contribution

BCF=(0,1).

Then

P_{\Ker A^{*}}BCF=(0,1)\neq 0.

Thus strict flatness alone does not imply Schur flatness.

There are, however, three mechanisms by which strict flatness could imply Schur cancellation in special situations.

(a)

Schur response equals a next strict obstruction modulo trace range.
If one proves

B_{K}C_{K}F_{K}\equiv\mathcal{O}_{K+1}^{\mathrm{str}}\pmod{\Range A_{K}},

then all-order strict flatness implies trace-projectability.

(b)

Surviving-branch equations impose amplitude ratios.
In the two-mode model, Schur cancellation requires the amplitude ratio (
B.29
). If the equations
\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}}=0
force that ratio, then Schur cancellation follows.

(c)

The Schur defect is itself a visible obstruction.
If
P_{\Ker A_{K}^{*}}B_{K}C_{K}F_{K}\neq 0
, it may have to be added to the visible obstruction space and removed before the next reduced quotient is formed.

This suggests a refinement of the obstruction hierarchy. In addition to strict compatibility obstructions
\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}}
, one should track Schur trace-projectability obstructions

\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{Schur}}=P_{\Ker A_{j}^{*}}B_{j}C_{j}F_{j}.

A genuinely surviving branch should be both strict-flat and Schur-flat:

\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}}=0,\qquad\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{Schur}}=0,\qquad\forall j.

(9.28)

Problem 9.8

(Strict–Schur obstruction hierarchy)
.

Develop a two-layer obstruction hierarchy in which finite-order strict compatibility obstructions and finite-order Schur trace-projectability obstructions are removed recursively. Prove that a failed branch either exhibits a visible obstruction of one of these two types, or else is strict–Schur flat to all finite orders and must be treated by a genuinely
\mathrm{NS}
-specific realizability principle or by a relaxed-shadow comparison framework.

9.8
Strict–Schur two-layer obstruction hierarchy

The preceding subsection shows that strict finite-mode flatness is not the same as trace-projectability of the full Navier–Stokes vertical response. We therefore refine the obstruction hierarchy by separating two layers:

\text{strict compatibility obstructions}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{Schur trace-projectability obstructions}.

A genuinely surviving branch should be flat with respect to both layers.

The first layer is the strict compatibility hierarchy. For a formal strict branch

V_{\eta}^{(K)}=V+\eta R_{1}+\eta^{2}R_{2}+\cdots+\eta^{K}R_{K},

write

\mathcal{C}(V_{\eta}^{(K)})=\sum_{j=1}^{K}\eta^{j}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\mathrm{str}}+\eta^{K+1}\mathcal{R}_{K+1}^{\mathrm{str}}(\eta).

(9.29)

In the zero-shadow quadratic model,
